Large corpus: 105 videos from 71 channels, 25 in the last 12 months and 56 older A large enough corpus for agreement across many independent channels to mean something.

With a corpus this shape, a position needs at least 6 older channels behind it before its absence from recent videos can be called expired. Anything backed by fewer is filed as fading, whatever it looks like. So "no expired advice" here means none was detectable, not that none exists.

The expired and current verdicts are tested on the 25 recent and 56 older videos that relevance search reached, not the full 105. The other 24 recent videos came only from a date-filtered search, which surfaces newer and more tactical material than relevance ranking ever returns for older years. Comparing those two directly would report that sampling difference as advice dying. All 105 videos still count towards support and weighting.

How to read this, and what it cannot tell you

Every claim is tied to the video that made it and weighted by that video's age, halving every 1.5 years. Support is counted in distinct channels, not videos, so one person uploading the same advice five times counts once.

Expired is the one that needs care. It means a position was well backed in older videos and is absent from recent ones by more than chance would explain, tested against how often we would expect to see it if it were still current, at a threshold of p < 0.1. Where the absence could plausibly be chance, the position is filed under fading instead and no claim is made.

This measures what creators say, not what is true. Popular advice and correct advice are different things, and a consensus of people copying each other is still only one idea. 3 videos could not be fetched or had no usable captions. Quotes come from automatic captions, which mangle names and jargon.

Settled

130 positions

Backed across both older and recent videos. This has survived a changing platform.

Choose one narrow market, and one that is able and willing to spend

Expertise in a single vertical outperforms being spread across many, makes prospecting methods work, and is credited with large revenue jumps. The market must also be able to pay: startups, solo operators and low-lifetime-value categories are named as choosing clients with no money. Thresholds cited disagree — a minimum client revenue, a minimum lifetime value, a minimum lead value, and company headcount bands from two to ten employees up to 51-200.

Trigger outreach off an observed signal rather than a static filter

Signals cited include job changes and promotions, hiring for the role your service replaces, funding, headcount jumps, competitor software in use, and people engaging with your own, a competitor's or an adjacent creator's posts. Newly appointed executives need early wins and are more open; timing windows range from the first month to 90 days. Social engagement was named the best-performing outbound trigger of one year, and one creator says referencing a recent post beat firmographic triggers contrary to expectation.

Market the result the buyer gets, ideally priced in money, never the service or mechanism

Buyers want the transformation, not sessions, reports, features or the technical build, and features must be tied to a stated benefit because prospects will not make the connection themselves. The strongest version puts a monetary figure on it — a per-unit saving multiplied into an annual number, a percentage of funds raised, or the profit value of the jobs a retainer will generate.

Define the ideal customer profile before prospecting, and keep refining it from who actually replies

The hard part is finding the right leads, not leads. Criteria set too broad make the list irrelevant, too narrow collapse the market, and contacting everyone tanks reply rates. Supporters disagree on the basis: some use firmographics, others insist behaviour and motivation beat job title, size and industry. Several rebuild lists around the shared traits of positive responders, one citing a move from 3% to 10% reply.

Cold calling is a volume-and-repetition skill with knowable dial-to-meeting ratios

Competence comes only from dialling daily over a long period: confidence follows action, the first dials of a session are the worst, nerves resolve after a few dozen calls, and no sale should be expected in the first week or month. Prescriptions range from a fixed 30 minutes a day to six to eight hours. Benchmark ratios disagree widely — 40 dials an hour, 50 dials to five conversations, 25 dials per meeting, 100 calls to 15 conversations and 2 bookings, three to five meetings a day — with more than 80% of calls said to be lost in the first 60-90 seconds and a conversation rate below 5% signalling a broken call rather than a bad list.

Benchmark rates exist, and which one you miss tells you which element is broken

Cited targets disagree: 1.5-3% reply as healthy, 5-10% as the target zone, 11.5% from a custom-scraped list, 40-60% open with below 30% signalling deliverability trouble, 6% open and under 1% reply as the industry average, and one booked call per 200 or per 500 emails. The diagnostic reading is that a low open rate points at the subject line and deliverability, a reply rate below about 2% at the list or the domains, and 2-5% at the copy and offer.

Patrick Dang · 2020-09 · 7 Keys to Lead Generation & Sales Prospecting for Business Develop →
Put a qualification step in front of every salesperson's calendar and make prospects prove they fit

Raw meeting counts are meaningless without it — fifty meetings that produce no buyers are worthless, activity volume is a vanity metric when the people contacted could never buy, and a lead who never opens the follow-up converts at zero. The positioning version inverts the sale: open by saying you need to check fit, state criteria that let them self-select, and close by telling them they qualify rather than asking if they want to move forward. It only works if the selectivity is genuine and you do not need every deal.

Never ask directly whether they are interested; invert the ask so they pull you back in

The direct question reduces the call to a yes or no, triggers reactance, and no is the only rational answer at that stage. Inversions cited include saying you are not sure it makes sense to talk, predicting they already have it handled, making a deliberately wrong assumption so they correct you, attributing reasons to other people, and asking whether they would be opposed rather than open — the aim being that the meeting becomes their own idea.

The free or cheap front end must expose the next problem, not solve the paid one

Sell at the point of greatest deprivation — a trial that gives the solution then removes it, or a diagnostic revealing a problem that worsens with delay. Give the strategy away and sell the execution. A very low-priced introductory offer works the same way, converting people who would never buy the main product and flipping them psychologically from prospect to customer; break even on the front end and profit from the fraction who take the back end.

There is a minimum budget below which the algorithm cannot learn, and the account must be left alone to spend it properly

Incentives only change behaviour above a threshold — discounts and partner commissions must be big enough to matter

Discount a fixed amount rather than a percentage so margin does not scale down, cap it at the first N bookings, and note that discounts below about 50% are said not to change buyer behaviour. Standard 20% affiliate commissions are likewise said not to change partner behaviour; carving off a front-end piece the partner keeps entirely does. Referral schemes are described as lower risk than paid ads because you only pay once revenue arrives, and rewards can be status or access rather than cash.
